Honestly, it’s hard for me to separate the two anymore. I came in because I’m barely sleeping in any way that feels useful. Even when I’m exhausted, I’ll get into bed and my brain just starts replaying everything from work — client models, assumptions I used, numbers I should double-check, what someone might ask me the next day, whether I missed something.

I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, already feeling like I’m behind, like the day has started before I’ve even had a chance to rest. So by the time I get to work, I feel like I never actually slept. I’m not sure I can really say what I’m like after a “good” night, because I don’t feel like I’ve had one in a while.

It doesn’t really calm down the way I would expect it to. I mean, once I’m up and at work, I’m busy, yes, but it’s not like the worry turns off — it sort of gets absorbed into whatever I’m doing.

The thing I notice most is that my thinking feels… slower, or less reliable. Spreadsheets that used to be almost automatic for me now take forever. I’ll be looking at assumptions for a client model, and I reread the same line or the same input over and over because I lose the thread of what I was checking. Then I’ll think, wait, did I already verify that? Did I use the right number? And I’ll go back again.

Before presentations it’s especially bad, because even after I’ve checked something multiple times, I still have this feeling that I must have missed something obvious. So I’m not exactly calm just because I’m busy. If anything, being busy gives my mind more things to get stuck on.

Spreadsheets that used to be almost automatic for me now take forever. I’ll be looking at assumptions for a client model, and I reread the same line or the same input over and over because I lose the thread of what I was checking. I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… I know I worry, but when you say outside of work and sleep, I’m not sure I can cleanly separate it like that.

Most of what’s been taking up space in my head is work, and then the sleep problem becomes its own thing because I’m lying there thinking about work, and then I’m worried about not sleeping, and then I’m worried about how I’ll function the next day. So it all kind of loops together.

I’m trying to think about that carefully, because I don’t want to just say yes because it sounds like the expected answer. I guess… I don’t know that I’ve clearly noticed that kind of background worry outside of work. Not in a way I could point to and say, yes, that’s happening most days even when everything is fine.

Even when I’m exhausted, I’ll get into bed and my brain just starts replaying everything from work — client models, assumptions I used, numbers I should double-check, what someone might ask me the next day, whether I missed something.\n\nAnd then if I do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like it lasts. I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, already feeling like I’m behind, like the day has started before I’ve even had a chance to rest. So by the time I get to work, I feel like I never actually slept. I’m not sure I can really say what I’m like after a “good” night, because I don’t feel like I’ve had one in a while.\n\nDoctor: When you're awake during the day, do you find your mind still racing with these worries even when you're not thinking about sleep, or does it tend to calm down once you're up and busy with your day?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t really calm down the way I would expect it to. I mean, once I’m up and at work, I’m busy, yes, but it’s not like the worry turns off — it sort of gets absorbed into whatever I’m doing.\n\nThe thing I notice most is that my thinking feels… slower, or less reliable. Spreadsheets that used to be almost automatic for me now take forever. I’ll be looking at assumptions for a client model, and I reread the same line or the same input over and over because I lose the thread of what I was checking. Then I’ll think, wait, did I already verify that? Did I use the right number? And I’ll go back again.\n\nBefore presentations it’s especially bad, because even after I’ve checked something multiple times, I still have this feeling that I must have missed something obvious. So I’m not exactly calm just because I’m busy. If anything, being busy gives my mind more things to get stuck on.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and sleep-related concerns, do you find yourself worrying about other things in your life, like relationships, health, or everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… I know I worry, but when you say outside of work and sleep, I’m not sure I can cleanly separate it like that.\n\nMost of what’s been taking up space in my head is work, and then the sleep problem becomes its own thing because I’m lying there thinking about work, and then I’m worried about not sleeping, and then I’m worried about how I’ll function the next day. So it all kind of loops together.\n\nI don’t know that I’ve noticed myself sitting around worrying in the same way about, say, random everyday things. Or maybe I have and I’m just not recognizing it because the work stuff is so loud right now. I’m not trying to dodge the question — I just don’t have a clear answer beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling anxious or worried about things outside of work, even if it's subtle—like a background sense of unease or tension—on most days, even when things seem to be going fine?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about that carefully, because I don’t want to just say yes because it sounds like the expected answer. I guess… I don’t know that I’ve clearly noticed that kind of background worry outside of work. Not in a way I could point to and say, yes, that’s happening most days even when everything is fine.\n\nI mean, I can feel tense, but when I trace it back, it usually seems connected to something work-related or to whether I’m going to be able to sleep and function. If things are genuinely calm — which, honestly, I’m not sure how often that is — I don’t know that I’m sitting there worrying about other parts of life in the same way.\n\nMaybe there’s some general unease underneath it all, but I’m not confident I can separate that from being worn down by the work and sleep loop. Yes… mostly it shows up around sleep, or what’s left of sleep. I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body is exhausted, but my mind just keeps replaying client models — assumptions, inputs, what I might have missed — and I’ll lie there for hours feeling wound up.

And then even if I do fall asleep, I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, like I’ve already been bracing for the day before the day has even started. I wouldn’t necessarily describe it as a racing heart every time, but physically I feel clenched and unrested. Then I go to work feeling like I never actually slept, like my body was lying down but nothing was restored.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed it in that broad way you’re describing.

It’s not like I’m walking around worrying about everything — like my health, or my relationships, or the house, or all the ordinary life things — with no clear reason. When I feel worried, I can usually trace it back to work, or to the sleep issue, and then those two feed into each other. That’s the part I can identify.

I guess I’m hesitating because I do feel worn down and on edge a lot, so maybe from the outside it looks like general anxiety. But if you’re asking whether my mind is jumping from one unrelated concern to another all day, even when nothing is going wrong, I don’t really think that’s what’s happening. Or at least I’m not aware of it happening. It feels more concentrated than that, even if it takes over a lot of space.

I mean, once I’m up and at work, I’m busy, yes, but it’s not like the worry turns off — it sort of gets absorbed into whatever I’m doing.\n\nThe thing I notice most is that my thinking feels… slower, or less reliable. Yes. I mean… yes, definitely. Over the past two weeks, most days have had this heavy, gray feeling over them. It’s not always dramatic in the sense that I’m visibly falling apart, but it’s there almost constantly, like I’m moving through everything on autopilot.

Even in my apartment, it’s the same. I’ll get home and everything just feels flat and heavy, like there’s no real separation between work and home emotionally.

And sometimes someone will ask me something completely ordinary, like how my day is going or whether I want to join a conversation, and I feel this sudden pressure in my throat, like I might cry if I have to respond like a normal person. I usually hold it together, but it takes effort. So yes… feeling down is definitely there most days.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I have. And I think that’s one of the parts that scares me, because those were the things that made me feel like I had a life outside of work.

I used to volunteer teach — just a small program, nothing glamorous, but it mattered to me. I liked explaining things, helping people feel less intimidated. And I’ve stopped showing up. At first I told myself I was just behind at work, or too tired that week, or I’d go back once things settled down. But then it became easier to cancel, and then easier not to answer, and now I feel embarrassed because it was something I actually cared about.

Same with yoga. I wasn’t some intensely disciplined person, but going gave my week a little structure, and I felt better afterward, or at least more like a person. Now I don’t go. I think about going and it feels like another obligation I can’t manage.

And book club too. That was one of the only social things I had that didn’t revolve around work. I liked reading, I liked the discussion, even if I didn’t always talk much. But lately I just… don’t have the energy to read, and the idea of sitting there pretending to be engaged feels impossible. So I’ve stopped going to that as well.

It’s also affecting work, which I know isn’t exactly what you asked, but it’s all tied together. I’ve actually had a formal performance warning because my models and presentations keep slipping. That was humiliating. I’m supposed to be the person who catches details, who makes clean models and clear presentations, and now things are getting missed. So yes, I’ve lost interest, but it’s more than that — the pieces of my life that used to hold me together are kind of falling away.

I’m not trying to dodge the question — I just don’t have a clear answer beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling anxious or worried about things outside of work, even if it's subtle—like a background sense of unease or tension—on most days, even when things seem to be going fine?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about that carefully, because I don’t want to just say yes because it sounds like the expected answer. I guess… I don’t know that I’ve clearly noticed that kind of background worry outside of work. Not in a way I could point to and say, yes, that’s happening most days even when everything is fine.\n\nI mean, I can feel tense, but when I trace it back, it usually seems connected to something work-related or to whether I’m going to be able to sleep and function. If things are genuinely calm — which, honestly, I’m not sure how often that is — I don’t know that I’m sitting there worrying about other parts of life in the same way.\n\nMaybe there’s some general unease underneath it all, but I’m not confident I can separate that from being worn down by the work and sleep loop. So… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure, and I haven’t really noticed it clearly outside of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms, like restlessness, muscle tension, fatigue, or a racing heart, especially when you're feeling anxious or stressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly it shows up around sleep, or what’s left of sleep. I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body is exhausted, but my mind just keeps replaying client models — assumptions, inputs, what I might have missed — and I’ll lie there for hours feeling wound up.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, like I’ve already been bracing for the day before the day has even started. I wouldn’t necessarily describe it as a racing heart every time, but physically I feel clenched and unrested. Then I go to work feeling like I never actually slept, like my body was lying down but nothing was restored.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about a variety of different things, not just work or sleep, even when there isn't a clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed it in that broad way you’re describing.\n\nIt’s not like I’m walking around worrying about everything — like my health, or my relationships, or the house, or all the ordinary life things — with no clear reason. When I feel worried, I can usually trace it back to work, or to the sleep issue, and then those two feed into each other. That’s the part I can identify.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because I do feel worn down and on edge a lot, so maybe from the outside it looks like general anxiety. But if you’re asking whether my mind is jumping from one unrelated concern to another all day, even when nothing is going wrong, I don’t really think that’s what’s happening. Or at least I’m not aware of it happening. It feels more concentrated than that, even if it takes over a lot of space.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself feeling down, depressed, or losing interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, definitely. Over the past two weeks, most days have had this heavy, gray feeling over them. It’s not always dramatic in the sense that I’m visibly falling apart, but it’s there almost constantly, like I’m moving through everything on autopilot.\n\nAt the office, I’ll be sitting in meetings or walking between desks, and I’m doing what I’m supposed to do, answering people, looking at spreadsheets, trying to seem normal — but inside I feel like I’m just going through the motions. Even in my apartment, it’s the same. I’ll get home and everything just feels flat and heavy, like there’s no real separation between work and home emotionally.\n\nAnd sometimes someone will ask me something completely ordinary, like how my day is going or whether I want to join a conversation, and I feel this sudden pressure in my throat, like I might cry if I have to respond like a normal person. I usually hold it together, but it takes effort. So yes… feeling down is definitely there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve stopped enjoying or lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable or meaningful, like hobbies, time with friends, or other parts of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. And I think that’s one of the parts that scares me, because those were the things that made me feel like I had a life outside of work.\n\nI used to volunteer teach — just a small program, nothing glamorous, but it mattered to me. I liked explaining things, helping people feel less intimidated. And I’ve stopped showing up. At first I told myself I was just behind at work, or too tired that week, or I’d go back once things settled down. But then it became easier to cancel, and then easier not to answer, and now I feel embarrassed because it was something I actually cared about.\n\nSame with yoga. I wasn’t some intensely disciplined person, but going gave my week a little structure, and I felt better afterward, or at least more like a person. Now I don’t go. I think about going and it feels like another obligation I can’t manage.\n\nAnd book club too. That was one of the only social things I had that didn’t revolve around work. I liked reading, I liked the discussion, even if I didn’t always talk much. But lately I just… don’t have the energy to read, and the idea of sitting there pretending to be engaged feels impossible. So I’ve stopped going to that as well.\n\nIt’s also affecting work, which I know isn’t exactly what you asked, but it’s all tied together. I’ve actually had a formal performance warning because my models and presentations keep slipping. That was humiliating. I’m supposed to be the person who catches details, who makes clean models and clear presentations, and now things are getting missed. So yes, I’ve lost interest, but it’s more than that — the pieces of my life that used to hold me together are kind of falling away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

So yes, I’ve lost interest, but it’s more than that — the pieces of my life that used to hold me together are kind of falling away.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt down or lost interest in things almost every day, and if so, does it last throughout most of the day, even when nothing specific is wrong?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 48 / financial_or_investment_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most days I move through the office and even my apartment with this heavy, gray feeling, like I'm just going through the motions and trying not to cry when someone asks me a normal question.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… almost every day. I wish I could say it comes and goes more clearly, like there are good stretches where I feel like myself and then bad patches, but lately it’s more like this heavy grayness that’s just there for most of the day.

At work, I can still technically function — I mean, I show up, I sit at my desk, I answer emails, I go through meetings — but it feels like I’m acting out the part of a normal person rather than actually being one. I’ll be walking through the office or sitting with a spreadsheet in front of me, and everything feels muted, like I’m behind glass or something. I’m doing the motions, but there’s no real feeling of being connected to any of it.

And it doesn’t really lift when I get home, which is the part that makes it feel so constant. My apartment should feel like a break, but it doesn’t. I’m still carrying that same heaviness around, just in a different room. Sometimes nothing specific has happened at all — no argument, no crisis, no obvious reason — and I’ll still feel like I’m on the edge of crying. Someone can ask me a completely normal question, even something simple, and I have to sort of brace myself because I feel that pressure in my throat like if I answer too honestly I might fall apart.

So yes, it’s most days, and yes, it lasts through a lot of the day. It’s not always equally intense every minute, but it’s there enough that it feels like the background of everything.

I mean… I know I worry, but when you say outside of work and sleep, I’m not sure I can cleanly separate it like that.\n\nMost of what’s been taking up space in my head is work, and then the sleep problem becomes its own thing because I’m lying there thinking about work, and then I’m worried about not sleeping, and then I’m worried about how I’ll function the next day. I’m not trying to dodge the question — I just don’t have a clear answer beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling anxious or worried about things outside of work, even if it's subtle—like a background sense of unease or tension—on most days, even when things seem to be going fine?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about that carefully, because I don’t want to just say yes because it sounds like the expected answer. I guess… I don’t know that I’ve clearly noticed that kind of background worry outside of work. Not in a way I could point to and say, yes, that’s happening most days even when everything is fine.\n\nI mean, I can feel tense, but when I trace it back, it usually seems connected to something work-related or to whether I’m going to be able to sleep and function. If things are genuinely calm — which, honestly, I’m not sure how often that is — I don’t know that I’m sitting there worrying about other parts of life in the same way.\n\nMaybe there’s some general unease underneath it all, but I’m not confident I can separate that from being worn down by the work and sleep loop. So… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure, and I haven’t really noticed it clearly outside of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms, like restlessness, muscle tension, fatigue, or a racing heart, especially when you're feeling anxious or stressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly it shows up around sleep, or what’s left of sleep. I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body is exhausted, but my mind just keeps replaying client models — assumptions, inputs, what I might have missed — and I’ll lie there for hours feeling wound up.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, like I’ve already been bracing for the day before the day has even started. I wouldn’t necessarily describe it as a racing heart every time, but physically I feel clenched and unrested. Then I go to work feeling like I never actually slept, like my body was lying down but nothing was restored.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about a variety of different things, not just work or sleep, even when there isn't a clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed it in that broad way you’re describing.\n\nIt’s not like I’m walking around worrying about everything — like my health, or my relationships, or the house, or all the ordinary life things — with no clear reason. When I feel worried, I can usually trace it back to work, or to the sleep issue, and then those two feed into each other. And I think that’s one of the parts that scares me, because those were the things that made me feel like I had a life outside of work.\n\nI used to volunteer teach — just a small program, nothing glamorous, but it mattered to me. I liked explaining things, helping people feel less intimidated. And I’ve stopped showing up. At first I told myself I was just behind at work, or too tired that week, or I’d go back once things settled down. I’ll be walking through the office or sitting with a spreadsheet in front of me, and everything feels muted, like I’m behind glass or something. I’m doing the motions, but there’s no real feeling of being connected to any of it.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really lift when I get home, which is the part that makes it feel so constant. Someone can ask me a completely normal question, even something simple, and I have to sort of brace myself because I feel that pressure in my throat like if I answer too honestly I might fall apart.\n\nSo yes, it’s most days, and yes, it lasts through a lot of the day. I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who is constantly worrying about every little thing in that way, if that makes sense.

I mean, I do have thoughts running through my head a lot, but it’s hard for me to separate what’s “worry” from just feeling heavy or overwhelmed. I’ll go to bed exhausted, but then I just lie there with my mind running through work things—client models, assumptions, numbers, what I might have missed, what someone might question the next morning—and I can’t seem to shut it off.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’m waking up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, like I’m already behind before the day has even started. By the time I get to work, I feel like I never actually rested. I’m functioning, I guess, because I have to, but I feel wrung out before the day begins.\n\nDoctor: When you’re able to get a good night’s rest, do you still find yourself feeling anxious or overwhelmed during the day, or does your anxiety mostly come up around bedtime or sleep difficulties?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it’s hard for me to separate the two anymore. I came in because I’m barely sleeping in any way that feels useful. Even when I’m exhausted, I’ll get into bed and my brain just starts replaying everything from work — client models, assumptions I used, numbers I should double-check, what someone might ask me the next day, whether I missed something.\n\nAnd then if I do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like it lasts. I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, already feeling like I’m behind, like the day has started before I’ve even had a chance to rest. So by the time I get to work, I feel like I never actually slept. I’m not sure I can really say what I’m like after a “good” night, because I don’t feel like I’ve had one in a while.\n\nDoctor: When you're awake during the day, do you find your mind still racing with these worries even when you're not thinking about sleep, or does it tend to calm down once you're up and busy with your day?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t really calm down the way I would expect it to. I mean, once I’m up and at work, I’m busy, yes, but it’s not like the worry turns off — it sort of gets absorbed into whatever I’m doing.\n\nThe thing I notice most is that my thinking feels… slower, or less reliable. Spreadsheets that used to be almost automatic for me now take forever. I’ll be looking at assumptions for a client model, and I reread the same line or the same input over and over because I lose the thread of what I was checking. Then I’ll think, wait, did I already verify that? Did I use the right number? And I’ll go back again.\n\nBefore presentations it’s especially bad, because even after I’ve checked something multiple times, I still have this feeling that I must have missed something obvious. So I’m not exactly calm just because I’m busy. If anything, being busy gives my mind more things to get stuck on.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and sleep-related concerns, do you find yourself worrying about other things in your life, like relationships, health, or everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… I know I worry, but when you say outside of work and sleep, I’m not sure I can cleanly separate it like that.\n\nMost of what’s been taking up space in my head is work, and then the sleep problem becomes its own thing because I’m lying there thinking about work, and then I’m worried about not sleeping, and then I’m worried about how I’ll function the next day. So it all kind of loops together.\n\nI don’t know that I’ve noticed myself sitting around worrying in the same way about, say, random everyday things. Or maybe I have and I’m just not recognizing it because the work stuff is so loud right now. I’m not trying to dodge the question — I just don’t have a clear answer beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling anxious or worried about things outside of work, even if it's subtle—like a background sense of unease or tension—on most days, even when things seem to be going fine?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about that carefully, because I don’t want to just say yes because it sounds like the expected answer. I guess… I don’t know that I’ve clearly noticed that kind of background worry outside of work. Not in a way I could point to and say, yes, that’s happening most days even when everything is fine.\n\nI mean, I can feel tense, but when I trace it back, it usually seems connected to something work-related or to whether I’m going to be able to sleep and function. If things are genuinely calm — which, honestly, I’m not sure how often that is — I don’t know that I’m sitting there worrying about other parts of life in the same way.\n\nMaybe there’s some general unease underneath it all, but I’m not confident I can separate that from being worn down by the work and sleep loop. So… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure, and I haven’t really noticed it clearly outside of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms, like restlessness, muscle tension, fatigue, or a racing heart, especially when you're feeling anxious or stressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly it shows up around sleep, or what’s left of sleep. I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body is exhausted, but my mind just keeps replaying client models — assumptions, inputs, what I might have missed — and I’ll lie there for hours feeling wound up.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, like I’ve already been bracing for the day before the day has even started. I wouldn’t necessarily describe it as a racing heart every time, but physically I feel clenched and unrested. Then I go to work feeling like I never actually slept, like my body was lying down but nothing was restored.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about a variety of different things, not just work or sleep, even when there isn't a clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed it in that broad way you’re describing.\n\nIt’s not like I’m walking around worrying about everything — like my health, or my relationships, or the house, or all the ordinary life things — with no clear reason. When I feel worried, I can usually trace it back to work, or to the sleep issue, and then those two feed into each other. That’s the part I can identify.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because I do feel worn down and on edge a lot, so maybe from the outside it looks like general anxiety. But if you’re asking whether my mind is jumping from one unrelated concern to another all day, even when nothing is going wrong, I don’t really think that’s what’s happening. Or at least I’m not aware of it happening. It feels more concentrated than that, even if it takes over a lot of space.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself feeling down, depressed, or losing interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, definitely. Over the past two weeks, most days have had this heavy, gray feeling over them. It’s not always dramatic in the sense that I’m visibly falling apart, but it’s there almost constantly, like I’m moving through everything on autopilot.\n\nAt the office, I’ll be sitting in meetings or walking between desks, and I’m doing what I’m supposed to do, answering people, looking at spreadsheets, trying to seem normal — but inside I feel like I’m just going through the motions. Even in my apartment, it’s the same. I’ll get home and everything just feels flat and heavy, like there’s no real separation between work and home emotionally.\n\nAnd sometimes someone will ask me something completely ordinary, like how my day is going or whether I want to join a conversation, and I feel this sudden pressure in my throat, like I might cry if I have to respond like a normal person. I usually hold it together, but it takes effort. So yes… feeling down is definitely there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve stopped enjoying or lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable or meaningful, like hobbies, time with friends, or other parts of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. And I think that’s one of the parts that scares me, because those were the things that made me feel like I had a life outside of work.\n\nI used to volunteer teach — just a small program, nothing glamorous, but it mattered to me. I liked explaining things, helping people feel less intimidated. And I’ve stopped showing up. At first I told myself I was just behind at work, or too tired that week, or I’d go back once things settled down. But then it became easier to cancel, and then easier not to answer, and now I feel embarrassed because it was something I actually cared about.\n\nSame with yoga. I wasn’t some intensely disciplined person, but going gave my week a little structure, and I felt better afterward, or at least more like a person. Now I don’t go. I think about going and it feels like another obligation I can’t manage.\n\nAnd book club too. That was one of the only social things I had that didn’t revolve around work. I liked reading, I liked the discussion, even if I didn’t always talk much. But lately I just… don’t have the energy to read, and the idea of sitting there pretending to be engaged feels impossible. So I’ve stopped going to that as well.\n\nIt’s also affecting work, which I know isn’t exactly what you asked, but it’s all tied together. I’ve actually had a formal performance warning because my models and presentations keep slipping. That was humiliating. I’m supposed to be the person who catches details, who makes clean models and clear presentations, and now things are getting missed. So yes, I’ve lost interest, but it’s more than that — the pieces of my life that used to hold me together are kind of falling away.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or lost interest in things almost every day, and if so, does it last throughout most of the day, even when nothing specific is wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes… almost every day. I wish I could say it comes and goes more clearly, like there are good stretches where I feel like myself and then bad patches, but lately it’s more like this heavy grayness that’s just there for most of the day.\n\nAt work, I can still technically function — I mean, I show up, I sit at my desk, I answer emails, I go through meetings — but it feels like I’m acting out the part of a normal person rather than actually being one. I’ll be walking through the office or sitting with a spreadsheet in front of me, and everything feels muted, like I’m behind glass or something. I’m doing the motions, but there’s no real feeling of being connected to any of it.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really lift when I get home, which is the part that makes it feel so constant. My apartment should feel like a break, but it doesn’t. I’m still carrying that same heaviness around, just in a different room. Sometimes nothing specific has happened at all — no argument, no crisis, no obvious reason — and I’ll still feel like I’m on the edge of crying. Someone can ask me a completely normal question, even something simple, and I have to sort of brace myself because I feel that pressure in my throat like if I answer too honestly I might fall apart.\n\nSo yes, it’s most days, and yes, it lasts through a lot of the day. It’s not always equally intense every minute, but it’s there enough that it feels like the background of everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about a variety of everyday things — even small or routine matters — not just work or sleep, and even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. I mean… I do feel uncomfortable a lot of the time, but I don’t know if it’s “anxious” in the sense of feeling keyed up or like something bad is about to happen.

I’m functioning, I guess, because I have to, but I feel wrung out before the day begins.\n\nDoctor: When you’re able to get a good night’s rest, do you still find yourself feeling anxious or overwhelmed during the day, or does your anxiety mostly come up around bedtime or sleep difficulties?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it’s hard for me to separate the two anymore. I came in because I’m barely sleeping in any way that feels useful. Even when I’m exhausted, I’ll get into bed and my brain just starts replaying everything from work — client models, assumptions I used, numbers I should double-check, what someone might ask me the next day, whether I missed something.\n\nAnd then if I do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like it lasts. I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, already feeling like I’m behind, like the day has started before I’ve even had a chance to rest. So by the time I get to work, I feel like I never actually slept. I’m not sure I can really say what I’m like after a “good” night, because I don’t feel like I’ve had one in a while.\n\nDoctor: When you're awake during the day, do you find your mind still racing with these worries even when you're not thinking about sleep, or does it tend to calm down once you're up and busy with your day?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t really calm down the way I would expect it to. I mean, once I’m up and at work, I’m busy, yes, but it’s not like the worry turns off — it sort of gets absorbed into whatever I’m doing.\n\nThe thing I notice most is that my thinking feels… slower, or less reliable. Spreadsheets that used to be almost automatic for me now take forever. I’ll be looking at assumptions for a client model, and I reread the same line or the same input over and over because I lose the thread of what I was checking. Then I’ll think, wait, did I already verify that? Did I use the right number? And I’ll go back again.\n\nBefore presentations it’s especially bad, because even after I’ve checked something multiple times, I still have this feeling that I must have missed something obvious. So I’m not exactly calm just because I’m busy. If anything, being busy gives my mind more things to get stuck on.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and sleep-related concerns, do you find yourself worrying about other things in your life, like relationships, health, or everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… I know I worry, but when you say outside of work and sleep, I’m not sure I can cleanly separate it like that.\n\nMost of what’s been taking up space in my head is work, and then the sleep problem becomes its own thing because I’m lying there thinking about work, and then I’m worried about not sleeping, and then I’m worried about how I’ll function the next day. So it all kind of loops together.\n\nI don’t know that I’ve noticed myself sitting around worrying in the same way about, say, random everyday things. Or maybe I have and I’m just not recognizing it because the work stuff is so loud right now. I’m not trying to dodge the question — I just don’t have a clear answer beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling anxious or worried about things outside of work, even if it's subtle—like a background sense of unease or tension—on most days, even when things seem to be going fine?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about that carefully, because I don’t want to just say yes because it sounds like the expected answer. I guess… I don’t know that I’ve clearly noticed that kind of background worry outside of work. Not in a way I could point to and say, yes, that’s happening most days even when everything is fine.\n\nI mean, I can feel tense, but when I trace it back, it usually seems connected to something work-related or to whether I’m going to be able to sleep and function. If things are genuinely calm — which, honestly, I’m not sure how often that is — I don’t know that I’m sitting there worrying about other parts of life in the same way.\n\nMaybe there’s some general unease underneath it all, but I’m not confident I can separate that from being worn down by the work and sleep loop. So… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure, and I haven’t really noticed it clearly outside of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms, like restlessness, muscle tension, fatigue, or a racing heart, especially when you're feeling anxious or stressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly it shows up around sleep, or what’s left of sleep. I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body is exhausted, but my mind just keeps replaying client models — assumptions, inputs, what I might have missed — and I’ll lie there for hours feeling wound up.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, like I’ve already been bracing for the day before the day has even started. I wouldn’t necessarily describe it as a racing heart every time, but physically I feel clenched and unrested. Then I go to work feeling like I never actually slept, like my body was lying down but nothing was restored.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about a variety of different things, not just work or sleep, even when there isn't a clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed it in that broad way you’re describing.\n\nIt’s not like I’m walking around worrying about everything — like my health, or my relationships, or the house, or all the ordinary life things — with no clear reason. When I feel worried, I can usually trace it back to work, or to the sleep issue, and then those two feed into each other. That’s the part I can identify.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because I do feel worn down and on edge a lot, so maybe from the outside it looks like general anxiety. But if you’re asking whether my mind is jumping from one unrelated concern to another all day, even when nothing is going wrong, I don’t really think that’s what’s happening. Or at least I’m not aware of it happening. It feels more concentrated than that, even if it takes over a lot of space.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself feeling down, depressed, or losing interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, definitely. Over the past two weeks, most days have had this heavy, gray feeling over them. It’s not always dramatic in the sense that I’m visibly falling apart, but it’s there almost constantly, like I’m moving through everything on autopilot.\n\nAt the office, I’ll be sitting in meetings or walking between desks, and I’m doing what I’m supposed to do, answering people, looking at spreadsheets, trying to seem normal — but inside I feel like I’m just going through the motions. Even in my apartment, it’s the same. I’ll get home and everything just feels flat and heavy, like there’s no real separation between work and home emotionally.\n\nAnd sometimes someone will ask me something completely ordinary, like how my day is going or whether I want to join a conversation, and I feel this sudden pressure in my throat, like I might cry if I have to respond like a normal person. I usually hold it together, but it takes effort. So yes… feeling down is definitely there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve stopped enjoying or lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable or meaningful, like hobbies, time with friends, or other parts of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. And I think that’s one of the parts that scares me, because those were the things that made me feel like I had a life outside of work.\n\nI used to volunteer teach — just a small program, nothing glamorous, but it mattered to me. I liked explaining things, helping people feel less intimidated. And I’ve stopped showing up. At first I told myself I was just behind at work, or too tired that week, or I’d go back once things settled down. But then it became easier to cancel, and then easier not to answer, and now I feel embarrassed because it was something I actually cared about.\n\nSame with yoga. I wasn’t some intensely disciplined person, but going gave my week a little structure, and I felt better afterward, or at least more like a person. Now I don’t go. I think about going and it feels like another obligation I can’t manage.\n\nAnd book club too. That was one of the only social things I had that didn’t revolve around work. I liked reading, I liked the discussion, even if I didn’t always talk much. But lately I just… don’t have the energy to read, and the idea of sitting there pretending to be engaged feels impossible. So I’ve stopped going to that as well.\n\nIt’s also affecting work, which I know isn’t exactly what you asked, but it’s all tied together. I’ve actually had a formal performance warning because my models and presentations keep slipping. That was humiliating. I’m supposed to be the person who catches details, who makes clean models and clear presentations, and now things are getting missed. So yes, I’ve lost interest, but it’s more than that — the pieces of my life that used to hold me together are kind of falling away.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or lost interest in things almost every day, and if so, does it last throughout most of the day, even when nothing specific is wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes… almost every day. I wish I could say it comes and goes more clearly, like there are good stretches where I feel like myself and then bad patches, but lately it’s more like this heavy grayness that’s just there for most of the day.\n\nAt work, I can still technically function — I mean, I show up, I sit at my desk, I answer emails, I go through meetings — but it feels like I’m acting out the part of a normal person rather than actually being one. I’ll be walking through the office or sitting with a spreadsheet in front of me, and everything feels muted, like I’m behind glass or something. I’m doing the motions, but there’s no real feeling of being connected to any of it.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really lift when I get home, which is the part that makes it feel so constant. My apartment should feel like a break, but it doesn’t. I’m still carrying that same heaviness around, just in a different room. Sometimes nothing specific has happened at all — no argument, no crisis, no obvious reason — and I’ll still feel like I’m on the edge of crying. Someone can ask me a completely normal question, even something simple, and I have to sort of brace myself because I feel that pressure in my throat like if I answer too honestly I might fall apart.\n\nSo yes, it’s most days, and yes, it lasts through a lot of the day. It’s not always equally intense every minute, but it’s there enough that it feels like the background of everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about a variety of everyday things — even small or routine matters — not just work or sleep, and even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who is constantly worrying about every little thing in that way, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I do have thoughts running through my head a lot, but it’s hard for me to separate what’s “worry” from just feeling heavy or overwhelmed. If you mean, like, am I afraid something bad is going to happen with ordinary things — groceries, errands, phone calls, bills, little daily decisions — I don’t know that I’d describe it that way exactly.\n\nSometimes small things feel harder than they should, but I’m not sure it’s because I’m anxious about them. It’s more that everything feels like it takes too much effort. So… I guess I haven’t really noticed broad worrying across lots of everyday things. Not clearly, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge even when you're not thinking about work or sleep, like something bad might happen or that you're keyed up for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. I mean… I do feel uncomfortable a lot of the time, but I don’t know if it’s “anxious” in the sense of feeling keyed up or like something bad is about to happen.\n\nIf I’m not thinking about work or sleep, I’m usually more… flat, or weighed down, rather than on edge. Sometimes I’m tense just because I feel like I can’t quite relax, but I don’t have that clear feeling of danger or panic, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “Something terrible is going to happen.” It’s more like everything feels difficult and I don’t have much room inside me.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed that kind of free-floating anxiety. Or maybe I’m not recognizing it properly, but it doesn’t stand out to me the way the heaviness does.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d say I worry a lot about different things, no. Or… I’m hesitating because I do spend a lot of time in my head, but it doesn’t always feel like worry in the way people mean it.

Like, I’m not usually jumping from one concern to another — money, health, errands, people being upset with me, something going wrong — at least not in a clear way I can point to. It’s more that everything feels heavy and difficult, and then I can get stuck thinking about how hard it all feels. But I don’t necessarily have this list of specific fears or “what ifs” running all day.

Sometimes I wonder if I’m under-reporting it because I’m so used to feeling bad that I don’t label things very well anymore. But if you’re asking whether I’m worried all the time about lots of areas of my life without a reason, I don’t think that’s the main thing I notice. It’s more… I feel weighed down than worried, if that distinction makes sense.

So… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure, and I haven’t really noticed it clearly outside of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms, like restlessness, muscle tension, fatigue, or a racing heart, especially when you're feeling anxious or stressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly it shows up around sleep, or what’s left of sleep. I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body is exhausted, but my mind just keeps replaying client models — assumptions, inputs, what I might have missed — and I’ll lie there for hours feeling wound up.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, like I’ve already been bracing for the day before the day has even started. I wouldn’t necessarily describe it as a racing heart every time, but physically I feel clenched and unrested. Then I go to work feeling like I never actually slept, like my body was lying down but nothing was restored.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about a variety of different things, not just work or sleep, even when there isn't a clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed it in that broad way you’re describing.\n\nIt’s not like I’m walking around worrying about everything — like my health, or my relationships, or the house, or all the ordinary life things — with no clear reason. I guess… I don’t know that I’ve clearly noticed that kind of background worry outside of work. Not in a way I could point to and say, yes, that’s happening most days even when everything is fine.\n\nI mean, I can feel tense, but when I trace it back, it usually seems connected to something work-related or to whether I’m going to be able to sleep and function. If things are genuinely calm — which, honestly, I’m not sure how often that is — I don’t know that I’m sitting there worrying about other parts of life in the same way.\n\nMaybe there’s some general unease underneath it all, but I’m not confident I can separate that from being worn down by the work and sleep loop. So… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure, and I haven’t really noticed it clearly outside of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms, like restlessness, muscle tension, fatigue, or a racing heart, especially when you're feeling anxious or stressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly it shows up around sleep, or what’s left of sleep. I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body is exhausted, but my mind just keeps replaying client models — assumptions, inputs, what I might have missed — and I’ll lie there for hours feeling wound up.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, like I’ve already been bracing for the day before the day has even started. I wouldn’t necessarily describe it as a racing heart every time, but physically I feel clenched and unrested. Then I go to work feeling like I never actually slept, like my body was lying down but nothing was restored.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about a variety of different things, not just work or sleep, even when there isn't a clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed it in that broad way you’re describing.\n\nIt’s not like I’m walking around worrying about everything — like my health, or my relationships, or the house, or all the ordinary life things — with no clear reason. When I feel worried, I can usually trace it back to work, or to the sleep issue, and then those two feed into each other. But if you’re asking whether my mind is jumping from one unrelated concern to another all day, even when nothing is going wrong, I don’t really think that’s what’s happening. Or at least I’m not aware of it happening. I mean… yes, definitely. Over the past two weeks, most days have had this heavy, gray feeling over them. It’s not always dramatic in the sense that I’m visibly falling apart, but it’s there almost constantly, like I’m moving through everything on autopilot.\n\nAt the office, I’ll be sitting in meetings or walking between desks, and I’m doing what I’m supposed to do, answering people, looking at spreadsheets, trying to seem normal — but inside I feel like I’m just going through the motions. Even in my apartment, it’s the same. I’ll get home and everything just feels flat and heavy, like there’s no real separation between work and home emotionally.\n\nAnd sometimes someone will ask me something completely ordinary, like how my day is going or whether I want to join a conversation, and I feel this sudden pressure in my throat, like I might cry if I have to respond like a normal person. I usually hold it together, but it takes effort. And I think that’s one of the parts that scares me, because those were the things that made me feel like I had a life outside of work.\n\nI used to volunteer teach — just a small program, nothing glamorous, but it mattered to me. I liked explaining things, helping people feel less intimidated. And I’ve stopped showing up. At first I told myself I was just behind at work, or too tired that week, or I’d go back once things settled down. But then it became easier to cancel, and then easier not to answer, and now I feel embarrassed because it was something I actually cared about.\n\nSame with yoga. I wasn’t some intensely disciplined person, but going gave my week a little structure, and I felt better afterward, or at least more like a person. Now I don’t go. I think about going and it feels like another obligation I can’t manage.\n\nAnd book club too. That was one of the only social things I had that didn’t revolve around work. I liked reading, I liked the discussion, even if I didn’t always talk much. But lately I just… don’t have the energy to read, and the idea of sitting there pretending to be engaged feels impossible. So I’ve stopped going to that as well.\n\nIt’s also affecting work, which I know isn’t exactly what you asked, but it’s all tied together. I’ve actually had a formal performance warning because my models and presentations keep slipping. That was humiliating. I’m supposed to be the person who catches details, who makes clean models and clear presentations, and now things are getting missed. So yes, I’ve lost interest, but it’s more than that — the pieces of my life that used to hold me together are kind of falling away.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or lost interest in things almost every day, and if so, does it last throughout most of the day, even when nothing specific is wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes… almost every day. I wish I could say it comes and goes more clearly, like there are good stretches where I feel like myself and then bad patches, but lately it’s more like this heavy grayness that’s just there for most of the day.\n\nAt work, I can still technically function — I mean, I show up, I sit at my desk, I answer emails, I go through meetings — but it feels like I’m acting out the part of a normal person rather than actually being one. I’ll be walking through the office or sitting with a spreadsheet in front of me, and everything feels muted, like I’m behind glass or something. I’m doing the motions, but there’s no real feeling of being connected to any of it.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really lift when I get home, which is the part that makes it feel so constant. Someone can ask me a completely normal question, even something simple, and I have to sort of brace myself because I feel that pressure in my throat like if I answer too honestly I might fall apart.\n\nSo yes, it’s most days, and yes, it lasts through a lot of the day. It’s not always equally intense every minute, but it’s there enough that it feels like the background of everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about a variety of everyday things — even small or routine matters — not just work or sleep, and even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who is constantly worrying about every little thing in that way, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I do have thoughts running through my head a lot, but it’s hard for me to separate what’s “worry” from just feeling heavy or overwhelmed. It’s more that everything feels like it takes too much effort. So… I guess I haven’t really noticed broad worrying across lots of everyday things. I mean… I do feel uncomfortable a lot of the time, but I don’t know if it’s “anxious” in the sense of feeling keyed up or like something bad is about to happen.\n\nIf I’m not thinking about work or sleep, I’m usually more… flat, or weighed down, rather than on edge. Sometimes I’m tense just because I feel like I can’t quite relax, but I don’t have that clear feeling of danger or panic, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “Something terrible is going to happen.” It’s more like everything feels difficult and I don’t have much room inside me.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed that kind of free-floating anxiety. Yes… yes, that does happen. It’s actually one of the things that makes it hard for me to explain, because sometimes nothing has really happened yet and I already feel awful.

Like I’ll be in my apartment, or walking through the office, and it’s just this heavy, gray feeling over everything. Not always dramatic, exactly, but it’s there most days. I can be doing completely ordinary things — getting ready, sitting at my desk, going to get coffee, answering an email — and I feel like I’m just moving through the motions. Like I’m performing being a normal person.

And sometimes someone will ask me something really simple, like a normal work question, and I have this sudden feeling that I might cry, even though the question isn’t upsetting. It’s embarrassing, because from the outside nothing is wrong in that moment. But inside it feels like I’m carrying something heavy around all the time, and I’m trying very hard not to let it show.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F41.1, F43.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my sleep has gotten… honestly, it doesn’t feel like sleep anymore, not in any useful way. I’ll go to bed exhausted, but then I just lie there with my mind running through work things—client models, assumptions, numbers, what I might have missed, what someone might question the next morning—and I can’t seem to shut it off.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’m waking up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, like I’m already behind before the day has even started. By the time I get to work, I feel like I never actually rested. I’m functioning, I guess, because I have to, but I feel wrung out before the day begins.\n\nDoctor: When you’re able to get a good night’s rest, do you still find yourself feeling anxious or overwhelmed during the day, or does your anxiety mostly come up around bedtime or sleep difficulties?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it’s hard for me to separate the two anymore. I came in because I’m barely sleeping in any way that feels useful. Even when I’m exhausted, I’ll get into bed and my brain just starts replaying everything from work — client models, assumptions I used, numbers I should double-check, what someone might ask me the next day, whether I missed something.\n\nAnd then if I do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like it lasts. I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, already feeling like I’m behind, like the day has started before I’ve even had a chance to rest. So by the time I get to work, I feel like I never actually slept. I’m not sure I can really say what I’m like after a “good” night, because I don’t feel like I’ve had one in a while.\n\nDoctor: When you're awake during the day, do you find your mind still racing with these worries even when you're not thinking about sleep, or does it tend to calm down once you're up and busy with your day?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t really calm down the way I would expect it to. I mean, once I’m up and at work, I’m busy, yes, but it’s not like the worry turns off — it sort of gets absorbed into whatever I’m doing.\n\nThe thing I notice most is that my thinking feels… slower, or less reliable. Spreadsheets that used to be almost automatic for me now take forever. I’ll be looking at assumptions for a client model, and I reread the same line or the same input over and over because I lose the thread of what I was checking. Then I’ll think, wait, did I already verify that? Did I use the right number? And I’ll go back again.\n\nBefore presentations it’s especially bad, because even after I’ve checked something multiple times, I still have this feeling that I must have missed something obvious. So I’m not exactly calm just because I’m busy. If anything, being busy gives my mind more things to get stuck on.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and sleep-related concerns, do you find yourself worrying about other things in your life, like relationships, health, or everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… I know I worry, but when you say outside of work and sleep, I’m not sure I can cleanly separate it like that.\n\nMost of what’s been taking up space in my head is work, and then the sleep problem becomes its own thing because I’m lying there thinking about work, and then I’m worried about not sleeping, and then I’m worried about how I’ll function the next day. So it all kind of loops together.\n\nI don’t know that I’ve noticed myself sitting around worrying in the same way about, say, random everyday things. Or maybe I have and I’m just not recognizing it because the work stuff is so loud right now. I’m not trying to dodge the question — I just don’t have a clear answer beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling anxious or worried about things outside of work, even if it's subtle—like a background sense of unease or tension—on most days, even when things seem to be going fine?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about that carefully, because I don’t want to just say yes because it sounds like the expected answer. I guess… I don’t know that I’ve clearly noticed that kind of background worry outside of work. Not in a way I could point to and say, yes, that’s happening most days even when everything is fine.\n\nI mean, I can feel tense, but when I trace it back, it usually seems connected to something work-related or to whether I’m going to be able to sleep and function. If things are genuinely calm — which, honestly, I’m not sure how often that is — I don’t know that I’m sitting there worrying about other parts of life in the same way.\n\nMaybe there’s some general unease underneath it all, but I’m not confident I can separate that from being worn down by the work and sleep loop. So… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure, and I haven’t really noticed it clearly outside of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms, like restlessness, muscle tension, fatigue, or a racing heart, especially when you're feeling anxious or stressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly it shows up around sleep, or what’s left of sleep. I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body is exhausted, but my mind just keeps replaying client models — assumptions, inputs, what I might have missed — and I’ll lie there for hours feeling wound up.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, like I’ve already been bracing for the day before the day has even started. I wouldn’t necessarily describe it as a racing heart every time, but physically I feel clenched and unrested. Then I go to work feeling like I never actually slept, like my body was lying down but nothing was restored.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about a variety of different things, not just work or sleep, even when there isn't a clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed it in that broad way you’re describing.\n\nIt’s not like I’m walking around worrying about everything — like my health, or my relationships, or the house, or all the ordinary life things — with no clear reason. When I feel worried, I can usually trace it back to work, or to the sleep issue, and then those two feed into each other. That’s the part I can identify.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because I do feel worn down and on edge a lot, so maybe from the outside it looks like general anxiety. But if you’re asking whether my mind is jumping from one unrelated concern to another all day, even when nothing is going wrong, I don’t really think that’s what’s happening. Or at least I’m not aware of it happening. It feels more concentrated than that, even if it takes over a lot of space.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself feeling down, depressed, or losing interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, definitely. Over the past two weeks, most days have had this heavy, gray feeling over them. It’s not always dramatic in the sense that I’m visibly falling apart, but it’s there almost constantly, like I’m moving through everything on autopilot.\n\nAt the office, I’ll be sitting in meetings or walking between desks, and I’m doing what I’m supposed to do, answering people, looking at spreadsheets, trying to seem normal — but inside I feel like I’m just going through the motions. Even in my apartment, it’s the same. I’ll get home and everything just feels flat and heavy, like there’s no real separation between work and home emotionally.\n\nAnd sometimes someone will ask me something completely ordinary, like how my day is going or whether I want to join a conversation, and I feel this sudden pressure in my throat, like I might cry if I have to respond like a normal person. I usually hold it together, but it takes effort. So yes… feeling down is definitely there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve stopped enjoying or lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable or meaningful, like hobbies, time with friends, or other parts of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. And I think that’s one of the parts that scares me, because those were the things that made me feel like I had a life outside of work.\n\nI used to volunteer teach — just a small program, nothing glamorous, but it mattered to me. I liked explaining things, helping people feel less intimidated. And I’ve stopped showing up. At first I told myself I was just behind at work, or too tired that week, or I’d go back once things settled down. But then it became easier to cancel, and then easier not to answer, and now I feel embarrassed because it was something I actually cared about.\n\nSame with yoga. I wasn’t some intensely disciplined person, but going gave my week a little structure, and I felt better afterward, or at least more like a person. Now I don’t go. I think about going and it feels like another obligation I can’t manage.\n\nAnd book club too. That was one of the only social things I had that didn’t revolve around work. I liked reading, I liked the discussion, even if I didn’t always talk much. But lately I just… don’t have the energy to read, and the idea of sitting there pretending to be engaged feels impossible. So I’ve stopped going to that as well.\n\nIt’s also affecting work, which I know isn’t exactly what you asked, but it’s all tied together. I’ve actually had a formal performance warning because my models and presentations keep slipping. That was humiliating. I’m supposed to be the person who catches details, who makes clean models and clear presentations, and now things are getting missed. So yes, I’ve lost interest, but it’s more than that — the pieces of my life that used to hold me together are kind of falling away.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or lost interest in things almost every day, and if so, does it last throughout most of the day, even when nothing specific is wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes… almost every day. I wish I could say it comes and goes more clearly, like there are good stretches where I feel like myself and then bad patches, but lately it’s more like this heavy grayness that’s just there for most of the day.\n\nAt work, I can still technically function — I mean, I show up, I sit at my desk, I answer emails, I go through meetings — but it feels like I’m acting out the part of a normal person rather than actually being one. I’ll be walking through the office or sitting with a spreadsheet in front of me, and everything feels muted, like I’m behind glass or something. I’m doing the motions, but there’s no real feeling of being connected to any of it.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really lift when I get home, which is the part that makes it feel so constant. My apartment should feel like a break, but it doesn’t. I’m still carrying that same heaviness around, just in a different room. Sometimes nothing specific has happened at all — no argument, no crisis, no obvious reason — and I’ll still feel like I’m on the edge of crying. Someone can ask me a completely normal question, even something simple, and I have to sort of brace myself because I feel that pressure in my throat like if I answer too honestly I might fall apart.\n\nSo yes, it’s most days, and yes, it lasts through a lot of the day. It’s not always equally intense every minute, but it’s there enough that it feels like the background of everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about a variety of everyday things — even small or routine matters — not just work or sleep, and even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who is constantly worrying about every little thing in that way, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I do have thoughts running through my head a lot, but it’s hard for me to separate what’s “worry” from just feeling heavy or overwhelmed. If you mean, like, am I afraid something bad is going to happen with ordinary things — groceries, errands, phone calls, bills, little daily decisions — I don’t know that I’d describe it that way exactly.\n\nSometimes small things feel harder than they should, but I’m not sure it’s because I’m anxious about them. It’s more that everything feels like it takes too much effort. So… I guess I haven’t really noticed broad worrying across lots of everyday things. Not clearly, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge even when you're not thinking about work or sleep, like something bad might happen or that you're keyed up for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. I mean… I do feel uncomfortable a lot of the time, but I don’t know if it’s “anxious” in the sense of feeling keyed up or like something bad is about to happen.\n\nIf I’m not thinking about work or sleep, I’m usually more… flat, or weighed down, rather than on edge. Sometimes I’m tense just because I feel like I can’t quite relax, but I don’t have that clear feeling of danger or panic, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “Something terrible is going to happen.” It’s more like everything feels difficult and I don’t have much room inside me.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed that kind of free-floating anxiety. Or maybe I’m not recognizing it properly, but it doesn’t stand out to me the way the heaviness does.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry a lot about different things in your life, even when there isn’t an obvious reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I worry a lot about different things, no. Or… I’m hesitating because I do spend a lot of time in my head, but it doesn’t always feel like worry in the way people mean it.\n\nLike, I’m not usually jumping from one concern to another — money, health, errands, people being upset with me, something going wrong — at least not in a clear way I can point to. It’s more that everything feels heavy and difficult, and then I can get stuck thinking about how hard it all feels. But I don’t necessarily have this list of specific fears or “what ifs” running all day.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I’m under-reporting it because I’m so used to feeling bad that I don’t label things very well anymore. But if you’re asking whether I’m worried all the time about lots of areas of my life without a reason, I don’t think that’s the main thing I notice. It’s more… I feel weighed down than worried, if that distinction makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood feels low or depressed even when there isn’t an immediate stressor or problem to explain it, like first thing in the morning before anything has gone wrong or on days when work isn’t particularly overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that does happen. It’s actually one of the things that makes it hard for me to explain, because sometimes nothing has really happened yet and I already feel awful.\n\nLike I’ll be in my apartment, or walking through the office, and it’s just this heavy, gray feeling over everything. Not always dramatic, exactly, but it’s there most days. I can be doing completely ordinary things — getting ready, sitting at my desk, going to get coffee, answering an email — and I feel like I’m just moving through the motions. Like I’m performing being a normal person.\n\nAnd sometimes someone will ask me something really simple, like a normal work question, and I have this sudden feeling that I might cry, even though the question isn’t upsetting. It’s embarrassing, because from the outside nothing is wrong in that moment. But inside it feels like I’m carrying something heavy around all the time, and I’m trying very hard not to let it show.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I don’t think so, not in that broad way. I mean, I do get preoccupied with things sometimes, but when you say personal health, relationships, routine responsibilities — like a range of everyday issues — that doesn’t quite sound like what’s happening for me.

I’m not usually sitting there thinking, “What if something is wrong with my health?” or “What if this relationship falls apart?” or “What if I forget to pay something?” over and over for no reason.
